, and introduce a family of monos which can produce any path relation.

Definition 5.8
.

For an ma-hypergraph
G
and a binary relation
R\subseteq\mathsf{out}(G)\times\mathsf{in}(G)
, a mono
p:G\to P
is called a
path extension
if
P
consists of
p[G]\cong G
, augmented by additional vertices and
\mathcal{P}
-labelled hyperedges such that there is a path from an output
y\in j
to an input
x\in i
if and only if
(y,x)\in R
.

It follows by construction that
R=R_{p}
. Note that there is more than one way to construct a path extension for a given
R
, but if
R=R_{p}=R_{q}
then
p\sim q
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
L\leftarrow K\rightarrow R
be a left-linear ma-rule in
\mathbf{Hyp}_{\Sigma}
,
p:G^{\prime}\to P
a path extension,
and
m:L\to P
a convex match. Then,
m
factors as
L\xrightarrow{m^{\prime}}G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p}P
,
and the convex rewrite of
G^{\prime}
at
m^{\prime}
extends to a convex rewriting step of
P
at
m
as follows

\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{P}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q}

(17)

and furthermore
q
is a path extension.

Proof.

Because
L
contains no
\mathcal{P}
-hyperedges, the image of every hyperedge in
L
under
m
must be in the image of
G^{\prime}
under
p
. Furthermore, by left-linearity
L
contains no isolated vertex, so every vertex in the image of
m
is in the image of
G^{\prime}
under
p
. Hence
m
factors as
p\circ m^{\prime}
, as required.

The top pushouts in (
17
) are constructed as a convex DPO rewriting step. The bottom-left pushout is constructed as a pushout complement, which exists because
m
satisfies the gluing conditions with respect to
K
, so
p
satisfies the gluing conditions with respect to
C^{\prime}
(which contains
K
). The bottom-right square is a pushout. This corresponds to the original rewrite
P\rightsquigarrow Q
by uniqueness of the pushout complement
D
.

It only remains to show that
q
is a path extension. This follows from the fact that any
\mathcal{P}
-hyperedges in the pushout yielding
Q
must come from
D
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
L\leftarrow K\rightarrow R
,
p
,
q
,
m^{\prime}
, and
n^{\prime}
be given as in Lemma
5.2
.
Then, for any mono
k:G^{\prime}\to G
such that
k\lesssim p
the convex rewriting of
G^{\prime}
at
m^{\prime}

extends to a convex rewriting of
G
at
k\circ m^{\prime}
as follows, where
l\lesssim q

\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n^{\prime}}
\textstyle{G^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H}

(18)

Proof.

The bottom pushout squares are constructed from the rewrite of
G^{\prime}
as in Construction
3.3
. We first need to show that
k\circ m^{\prime}
is convex. If that were not the case, there would be a path from an output of the image of
L
to an input in
G
. But then, since
k\lesssim p
, we have by Lemma
5.1
that
k\circ m^{\prime}\lesssim p\circ m^{\prime}
. But then there is a path from an output of the image of
L
in
P
to an input, which contradicts convexity of
m=p\circ m^{\prime}
. Hence
k\circ m^{\prime}
is convex.

It only remains to show that
l\lesssim q
. Inspecting the bottom pushout squares of (
17
), we note that, because of monogamy of
G^{\prime}
, it must be the case that any path from an output to an input of the image
G^{\prime}
in
P
must be in
C^{\prime}
. Hence, the same path will be in the image of
H^{\prime}
in
Q
, so
R_{p}\subseteq R_{q}
. By the symmetry of the DPO construction, it is also the case that
R_{q}\subseteq R_{p}
so
R_{p}=R_{q}
.

Applying the same argument to the bottom pushout squares of (
18
), we conclude that
R_{l}=R_{k}
. Since
k\lesssim p
, we have
R_{l}=R_{k}\subseteq R_{p}=R_{q}
, so
l\lesssim q
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

For ma-rules
L_{1}\leftarrow K_{1}\rightarrow R_{1}
and
L_{2}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow R_{2}
and an ma-pre-critical pair

(G^{\prime}_{1,1}\leftarrow J^{\prime})\leftsquigarrow(G^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow J^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow(G^{\prime}_{1,2}\leftarrow J^{\prime})
, let
k:G^{\prime}_{0}\to G_{0}

be a mono such that the induced matches
L_{1}\to G^{\prime}_{0}\to G_{0}
and
L_{2}\to G^{\prime}_{0}\to G_{0}
are convex and

p:G^{\prime}_{0}\to P_{0}
a path extension such that
k\lesssim p
. Then, we can obtain the following rewrites by extending
the critical pair along
k
and
p
, respectively

(G_{1,1}\leftarrow J)\leftsquigarrow(G_{0}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G_{2,1}\leftarrow J)\qquad(P_{1,1}\leftarrow K)\leftsquigarrow(P_{0}\leftarrow K)\rightsquigarrow(P_{2,1}\leftarrow K)

If the right branching is joinable by convex rewriting, then so is the left one.

Proof.

We apply essentially the same technique as the proof of Theorem
3.1
, except that we additionally need to show that, when we extend derivations from the critical pair
G^{\prime}_{0}
to the full graph
G_{0}
following Construction
3.3

\textstyle{L_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{L_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\dagger)}
\textstyle{G_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma_{1})}
\scriptstyle{(\delta_{1})}
\textstyle{G_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma_{2})}
\scriptstyle{(\delta_{2})}
\textstyle{G_{2}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{G_{n}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{n}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\gamma_{n})}
\scriptstyle{(\delta_{n})}
\textstyle{G_{n}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{0}}
\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{1}}
\textstyle{C_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{2}}
\textstyle{\dots}
\textstyle{G_{n}}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{n}}
\textstyle{J^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\epsilon_{2})}
\scriptstyle{(\epsilon_{n})}
\textstyle{C_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\epsilon_{1})}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

each of the matches
L_{i}\to G^{\prime}_{i}\to G_{i}
is convex.

If all the critical pairs in
\mathcal{R}
are path-joinable, then there exists a path-extension
p:G^{\prime}_{0}\to P_{0}
that path-covers
G_{0}^{\prime}\to G_{0}
and
(P_{1,1}\leftarrow I)\leftsquigarrow(P_{0}\leftarrow I)\rightsquigarrow(P_{1,2}\leftarrow I)
is joinable. First, we can apply Lemma
5.2
to translate a convex rewrite
(P_{0}\leftarrow I)\rightsquigarrow(P_{1,i}\leftarrow I)
to a convex rewrite
(G^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G^{\prime}_{1,i}\leftarrow J)
. We can then apply Lemma
5.3
to extend this to a convex rewrite
(G_{0}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G_{1,i}\leftarrow J)
. This yields a path-extension
q:G^{\prime}_{1,i}\to P_{2,i}
that path-covers
G^{\prime}_{1,i}\to G_{1,i}
, hence we can iterate this process to get a convex rewrite
(G_{1,i}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G_{2,i}\leftarrow J)
, and so on.

When we path-join the critical pair, we obtain
P_{m,1}\cong P_{n,2}
. If we remove all of the
\mathcal{P}
hyperedges (and nodes connected only to
\mathcal{P}
hyperedges), this will restrict to an isomorphism
G^{\prime}_{m,1}\cong G^{\prime}_{n,2}
, which in turn yields an isomorphism
G_{m,1}\cong G_{n,2}
. Hence the branching
(G_{1,1}\leftarrow J)\leftsquigarrow(G_{0}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G_{1,2}\leftarrow J)
is joinable by convex rewriting.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

Given ma-rules
L_{1}\leftarrow K_{1}\rightarrow R_{1}
and
L_{2}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow R_{2}
and an ma-pre-critical pair

(G^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow J)
, a maximal path relation is a binary relation
R\subseteq\mathsf{out}(G^{\prime}_{0})\times\mathsf{in}(G^{\prime}_{0})
such that

1.

a mono
m:G^{\prime}_{0}\to H
exists for an ma-hypergraph
H
with
R_{m}=R
,

2.

the induced matchings
L_{1}\to G^{\prime}_{0}\to H
and
L_{2}\to G^{\prime}_{0}\to H
are convex, and

3.

no relation satisfying (1) and (2) is a proper superset of
R
.

Definition 5.10
.

Given ma-rules
L_{1}\leftarrow K_{1}\rightarrow R_{1}
and
L_{2}\leftarrow K_{2}\rightarrow R_{2}
, an ma-pre-critical pair

(G^{\prime}_{1,1}\leftarrow J^{\prime})\leftsquigarrow(G^{\prime}_{0}\leftarrow J^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow(G^{\prime}_{1,2}\leftarrow J^{\prime})
is called path joinable
if for any maximal path relation
R
, there exists a path extension
p:G^{\prime}_{0}\to P
with
R_{p}=R
such that the branching

(P_{1,1}\leftarrow K)\leftsquigarrow(P_{0}\leftarrow K)\rightsquigarrow(P_{1,2}\leftarrow K)
obtained by lifting the two rewriting steps
in the critical pair along
p
is joinable by convex rewriting.

theorem 5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a convex DPOI rewriting system. If all ma-pre-critical pairs are path joinable, then
\mathcal{R}
is locally confluent.

Proof.

For any branching
(G_{1,1}\leftarrow J)\leftsquigarrow(G_{0}\leftarrow J)\rightsquigarrow(G_{1,2}\leftarrow J)
, we can find an ma-pre-critical pair based at
G_{0}^{\prime}
where the embedding
e:G^{\prime}_{0}\to G_{0}
has a path relation
R_{e}
satisfying conditions (1) and (2) in Definition
5.9
. Hence, there exists a path-extension
p:G^{\prime}_{0}\to P_{0}
where
R_{e}\subseteq R_{p}
and the associated branching is joinable by convex rewriting. Hence by Lemma
5.4
, the branching based at
G_{0}
is also joinable by convex rewriting. Therefore
\mathcal{R}
is locally confluent.
∎

The converse of this theorem is almost true, but with a small caveat that one needs to consider local confluence of ma-hypergraphs over the full signature
\Sigma+\mathcal{P}
containing the formal path generators, rather than just
\Sigma
.

theorem 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a convex DPOI rewriting system. If it is locally confluent for all ma-hypergraphs labelled by
\Sigma+\mathcal{P}
, then all ma-pre-critical pairs are path joinable.

The proof is immediate, since failing to join the path-extension of a ma-pre-critical pair witnesses a failure of local confluence. There is no reason
a priori
that the above theorem would hold just for ma-hypergraphs. Hence, one can see the inclusion of the formal path generators as a sort of ‘stabilisation’ of the theory that rules out certain degenerate cases of convex rewriting systems, such as those where certain paths never exist or can always be broken by rewriting.

Theorem
5.4
gives us an effective way to check local confluence. For an ma-pre-critical pair, we need to enumerate all the maximal path relations, and for each one, construct a path extension and check if it is joinable. While there could in principle be exponentially many of these for each critical pair, conditions (1)-(3) in Definition
5.9
rule many of them out. We will see this process in action in the case study in Section
6.2
.

6
Case studies

We close the paper by providing two positive examples of our confluence results. Both of them
concern ma-hypergraphs, distinguishing between left-connected systems and convex rewriting.

6.1
Left-connected and confluent: non-commutative bimonoids

First case study is an application of the results on left-connected systems, showing confluence of the theory
\mathbf{NB}
of non-commutative bimonoids
(Example
2.3
(c)
). Below is the interpretation of the theory as a DPO system

\langle\!\langle\ulcorner\mathcal{R}_{\textbf{NBiM}}\urcorner\rangle\!\rangle
, which was shown to be terminating in
[
6
]

\begin{array}[]{rrclrrcl}\mathbf{NB}_{1}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{64.13962pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBassocMultL.pdf}}}}&\!\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{26.64957pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/fourDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{63.68794pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBassocMultR.pdf}}}}&\mathbf{NB}_{2}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{65.043pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBassocComultL.pdf}}}}&\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{26.64957pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/fourDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{66.39806pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBassocComultR.pdf}}}}\\
\mathbf{NB}_{3}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{60.07443pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBunitMultDL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{25.74619pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/oneDotZeroOne.pdf}}}}&\mathbf{NB}_{4}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{56.91263pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBunitMultUL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{25.74619pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/oneDotZeroOne.pdf}}}}\\
\mathbf{NB}_{5}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{60.97781pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBcomultCounitDL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{25.74619pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/oneDotZeroOne.pdf}}}}&\mathbf{NB}_{6}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{61.4295pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBcomultCounitUL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{25.74619pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/oneDotZeroOne.pdf}}}}\\
\mathbf{NB}_{7}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{60.52612pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBmultcounitL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{37.94176pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBmultcounitR.pdf}}}}&\mathbf{NB}_{8}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{60.97781pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBunitcomultL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{27.10126pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/twoDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{37.03838pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBunitcomultR.pdf}}}}\\
\mathbf{NB}_{9}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{73.17337pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBbialgL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{26.64957pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/fourDotsFramed.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!\!\rightarrow&\!\!\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{73.17337pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBbialgR.pdf}}}}&\mathbf{NB}_{10}:=&\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{48.78226pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/NBboneL.pdf}}}}&\leftarrow\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{23.48775pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/emptyFrame.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\rightarrow&{\!\!\!\!\raisebox{1.00006pt}{\parbox{23.48775pt}{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale]{graffles/emptyFrame.pdf}}}}\!\!\!\!}\end{array}

Given that the system is terminating, it suffices to show local confluence. Observe that
\langle\!\langle\ulcorner\mathcal{R}_{\textbf{NBiM}}\urcorner\rangle\!\rangle
is left-connected: monogamy is ensured by the fact that it is in the image of
\langle\!\langle\ulcorner\cdot\urcorner\rangle\!\rangle
; strong connectedness and left-linearity hold by inspection of the set of rules. We can thus use Theorem
5.3
and local confluence follows from joinability of the ma-pre-critical pairs. Among them, the pairs without overlap of rule applications pose no problem: they are trivially joinable. One example is given below, with the middle grey graph acting as the interface for all depicted derivation steps

Thus we confine ourselves to analysing actual critical pairs, with overlapping rule applications. One such pair is given below, also involving rules
\mathbf{NB}_{1}
and
\mathbf{NB}_{9}
. Again, we show how it is joined, with the interface of each step drawn in the centre

Overall there are 22 critical pairs to consider. For each of them we only show the graph exhibiting the overlap. It is straightforward to check that the corresponding pairs are all joinable

We can thereby conclude that
\mathbf{NB}
is a confluent rewriting system. Since it is also terminating, equivalence of terms in
\mathbf{NB}
is decidable by means of rewriting. Note that, by virtue of Corollary
5.1
, the above pre-critical pair analysis can be automated.

6.2
A confluent, non-left-connected example

We now consider an example of a rewriting system that is not left-connected, and demonstrate a proof of confluence by means of path extensions. Let
\Sigma=\{f:2\to 2,g:1\to 0,h:0\to 1\}
, satisfying one equation

=
f
f
f
g
h

which translates into the following ma-rule

The rule strictly decreases the number of
f
-labelled hyperedges in a graph, so
\mathbf{NLC}
is clearly terminating. Hence, it suffices to check local confluence to prove that

\mathbf{NLC}
is confluent.

\mathbf{NLC}
has two types of ma-pre-critical pairs. The first type is a genuine critical pair

Each of these is joinable by convex rewriting. The 24 variations of (
19
) and (
20
) are all basically identical to these two cases, with the only difference being that one or more paths in the path extension is replaced by an output connected directly to an input. These are also all joinable, and hence the system
\mathbf{NLC}
is confluent.

7
Conclusions

The starting observation of this paper (Theorem
3.1
) is that the Knuth-Bendix property holds for DPOI rewriting.
As a consequence (Corollary
3.1
), confluence is decidable for terminating systems.

The relevance of this is two-fold. On the conceptual side, it puts graph rewriting in tight correspondence with term rewriting:
When considering rewriting with interfaces, confluence is decidable for graphs as it is for terms
[
35
]
,
while the appropriate notion of ground confluence is undecidable in both cases
[
33
,
44
]
.

On the side of applications, our result allows one to study confluence for string diagrams.
One consequence of Theorem
3.1
and of our previous work in
[
4
]
is that, for all those
symmetric monoidal theories including a special Frobenius structure, which are already commonplace in computer science
[
12
,
13
,
49
,
15
,
16
,
10
,
1
,
25
]
,
local confluence can be checked by means of critical pair analysis.
Moreover, confluence can be decided automatically when termination is guaranteed (Corollary
4.1
).
Analogous results on critical pairs hold for those theories that do not include a special Frobenius structure, albeit with
a few caveats.
More precisely, confluence is accomplished by two kinds of restrictions. The first choice is to curb the family of admissible rules
to left-connected systems (Theorem
5.3
): The notion of critical pair is substantially unchanged,
so that confluence can be decided automatically along the same lines as in the Frobenius case (Corollary
5.1
).
The second option is to restrict the family of admissible rewriting steps to convex matches, at the cost of checking
a larger family of critical pairs in order to include path extensions (Theorem
5.4
). The family is still computable,
as witnessed by the algorithm proposed in Appendix
B
, thus obtaining again confluence decidability for terminating systems.

Our results apply to a variety of other non-Frobenius theories, such as those in
[
37
,
28
,
24
]
.
In any case, in all the proposed scenarios, these decision procedures are amenable to implementation in string diagram
rewriting tools like Quantomatic

[
34
]
(via an encoding of hypergraphs) or directly in hypergraph-based rewriting tools.

Appendix A
Competing Interest Declaration

Competing interests: The authors declare none.

Appendix B
Enumeration of Maximal Path Relations

The case study in section
6.2
makes use of the following Python code for enumerating maximal path relations. The main function
find_extensions
takes as arguments

•

inputs
: a set of input vertices,

•

outputs
: a set of output vertices,

•

paths
: a set of pairs indicating a path connects the given input to the output in the pre-critical pair,

•

forbidden_paths
: a set of pairs indicating that a given path must
not
exist from an output to an input, due to convexity.

⬇
